**Counterevidence/rival:** Reservoir construction, ownership, transmission and industrial location could alter usable supply; theoretical national potential is not economically deployable capacity.

**Inference limit:** Partial-text inspection only; underlying model and supplements not rerun. Local scarcity does not identify why factories originally clustered there.

## IND-K08

Malm argues that large proposed reservoir schemes in the 1820s–1830s failed partly because their collective financing and coordination requirements conflicted with manufacturers’ arrangements.

**Cases:** IND-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** low. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S06: Fleeing the Flowing Commons: Robert Thom, Water Reservoir Schemes, and the Shift to Steam Power in Early Nineteenth-Century Britain](https://portal.research.lu.se/en/publications/fleeing-the-flowing-commons-robert-thom-water-reservoir-schemes-a/), author-repository abstract. Supports: Explicitly attributes non-realisation of reservoir schemes to coordination and collective funding.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Shared reservoir requires funding and allocation -> manufacturers cannot coordinate -> proposed expansion not built.
- **strongest rival:** Projects may be physically unreliable or financially unattractive even under feasible collective governance.
- **discriminating observation:** Scheme correspondence and cost/flow evidence showing viable projects blocked specifically by coordination, compared with realised schemes.
- **evidence sought found:** Searched full article; only IND-S06 author abstract acquired. It explicitly makes the coordination claim; scheme-level evidence not found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Both rivals fit non-building. Weak-access claim retained only as attributed hypothesis.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Physical reliability, projected demand, land rights and cost assumptions are alternative or interacting explanations.

**Inference limit:** Abstract-only evidence: claim is about the author’s interpretation, not an independent finding that water was consistently cheaper or every proposed scheme feasible.

## IND-K09

Allen’s explanation assigns British mechanisation to labour being expensive relative to capital and energy, and subsequent wider diffusion to improvements that reduced other input requirements.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S07: The British Industrial Revolution in Global Perspective: chapter 1 excerpt](https://assets.cambridge.org/97805218/68273/excerpt/9780521868273_excerpt.pdf), chapter 1 excerpt pp. 1–3. Supports: States the factor-price and later engineering-diffusion thesis directly.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** High relevant labour cost relative to machinery/energy -> expected cost saving -> investment in labour-saving inventions/adoption.
- **strongest rival:** Low-paid spinning labour, quality requirements and skill/market complements could motivate or constrain adoption independently.
- **discriminating observation:** Task- and location-matched wages, output quality and machine payback comparisons before invention, including nonadopters.
- **evidence sought found:** Read Allen excerpt IND-S07 and archival-pay critique IND-S08; high-wage formulation is clear but spinner wage surge is challenged.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Allen’s underlying calculations and later responses not inspected; international and local wages use different comparisons.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Task-specific female wages, regional heterogeneity, skills, markets and political power complicate the aggregate comparison.

**Inference limit:** Attribution of a hypothesis; introductory excerpt does not validate Allen’s profitability calculations or universal foreign non-viability.

## IND-K10

Humphries and Schneider’s reconstructed British hand-spinning pay does not show a general wage surge immediately before the spinning inventions; it challenges including these workers in an undifferentiated high-wage economy.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S08: Spinning the Industrial Revolution](https://ora.ox.ac.uk/objects/uuid%3A4f321519-a0b7-4879-a521-139cbca81b25/download_file?safe_filename=Humphries_and_Schneider_Spinning_the_industrial_revolution.pdf&type_of_work=Journal+article), accepted-manuscript pp. 15–16, 25–32 and 37–41. Supports: Documents archival construction, source-type differences and absence of the proposed sharp wage increase.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Reconstructed pay by source type does not show the proposed general pre-invention surge -> weakens the proposed general wage-surge premise. This does not identify the marginal labour-cost return to a particular machine or exclude wage incentives.
- **strongest rival:** Samples overrepresent institutional/part-time low-output labour, concealing a high-wage commercial cotton margin.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable full-time commercial cotton spinning records with counts and hours, versus institutional and other fibres.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S08 source definitions, records and regressions: no general surge, source-type gaps, sparse cotton observations; explicit limits preserved.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Wage levels, wage changes, daily earnings, hourly labour costs and returns to a particular machine are different measures. Sparse commercial cotton records and working-time assumptions leave the relevant marginal incentive unresolved; alternative invention motives in IND-S08 remain speculative.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Cotton data are scarce, fibres and worker populations differ, and daily earnings involve time assumptions; Allen’s later rejoinders are not assessed here.

**Inference limit:** A challenge to a particular wage premise, not proof that factor prices never mattered or causal identification of quality control as the alternative motive.

## IND-K11

In 1835 Ure publicly advocated factory mechanisation partly as a means to substitute cheaper labour and reduce dependence on independent skilled workers.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S09: The Philosophy of Manufactures (selected passages)](https://sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu/mod/1835ure.asp), extract paragraphs beginning “The principle of the factory system”, “By the infirmity”, and “In the infancy of mechanical engineering”; compiler original-page ranges 20–23, 29–31. Supports: Records contemporary managerial advocacy for skill substitution and tractability.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Skilled mechanics and machine operatives remained necessary; Ure is a partisan advocate whose welfare claims are not corroborated by this extract.

**Inference limit:** Shows a stated aspiration, not universal employer intention, successful complete deskilling or demonstrated worker benefit.

## IND-K12

Kelly and Ó Gráda trace a pathway from instrument-making skill and precision measurement to complex textile machinery and the machine tools needed for repeated production and replacement parts.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S10: Connecting the Scientific and Industrial Revolutions: The Role of Practical Mathematics](https://www.econstor.eu/bitstream/10419/228197/1/1701742357.pdf), sections 2.1–2.3, printed pp. 13–18 (PDF pp. 15–20). Supports: Identifies skill transfer, gauges and exact tools, and the demand for reproducible wearing components.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Precision craft/measurement capabilities -> reproducible complicated components -> machinery supply and replacement -> sustained industrial use.
- **strongest rival:** Demand/capital expansion alone induces both tool skill and machinery; capabilities could be an outcome rather than prior constraint.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated personnel/tool transfers preceding new workable designs and matched sites with demand but missing skills.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S10 sections 2.1–2.3: named instrument/mechanic transfers, valve making and machine-tool/parts account; also simpler early artifacts acknowledged.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Demand and supply reinforce one another; broad national claims lack an exogenous comparison.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The jenny and atmospheric engine could use less elaborate capabilities; cross-country tool histories have other routes and political contingencies.

**Inference limit:** A historically supported pathway rather than a universal prerequisite; no independent causal effect of national skill endowment is estimated.

## IND-K13

Broadberry and Gupta’s reconstruction makes the timing of Lancashire’s competitive gains depend on raw-cotton and transport costs as well as wages and uneven productivity advances across spinning and weaving.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S11: Cotton Textiles and the Great Divergence: Lancashire, India and Shifting Competitive Advantage, 1600–1850](https://muslintrust.org/wp-content/uploads/2018/01/wrap_broadberry-cotton-textiles-and-the-great-diverge.pdf), printed pp. 16–25, 27, 33; tables 7–13. Supports: Reconstructs comparative wages, productivity proxies, raw cotton prices and market-specific delivery costs.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Productivity rises unevenly and cotton/freight costs change -> relative delivered cost shifts -> merchants source differently -> competitive reversal.
- **strongest rival:** Colonial policy and coercive market access could drive the same trade shifts without equivalent cost advantage.
- **discriminating observation:** Quality-matched delivered costs and shares across markets with different policy but similar transport, before and after price changes.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S11 pp. 16–25 and conclusion; compared IND-S13 policy testimony. The cost model includes cotton/freight but assumes some Indian productivity paths.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Policy shapes prices and markets; quality and labour-unit comparability remain incomplete.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Indian productivity stagnation is assumed for key intervals, and household earnings and labour units are not directly comparable; political market access remains a rival/complement.

**Inference limit:** Applies to the inspected 2005 model and reconstruction, not directly measured productivity for all Indian textiles or proof of tariffs’ irrelevance.

## IND-K14

Clingingsmith and Williamson propose that pre-1810 Indian textile decline involved domestic political/agricultural shocks, while later decline involved stronger international manufactured-price pressure.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S12: Mughal Decline, Climate Change, and Britain’s Industrial Ascent: An Integrated Perspective on India’s 18th and 19th Century Deindustrialization](https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w11730/w11730.pdf), sections 4–7, printed pp. 19–28. Supports: Uses a three-sector model and relative-price evidence to propose two phases.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Domestic agricultural/political shock -> dear grain and subsistence-linked nominal wages -> reduced textile competitiveness; later world textile prices add pressure.
- **strongest rival:** Colonial interventions or textile demand shifts could alter prices and employment without the posited agricultural cause.
- **discriminating observation:** District timing: independently measured harvest/drought/conflict shocks preceding wage pressure and textile contraction, including low-shock controls.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S12 model and relative-price/timing argument; no independently identified district treatment comparison found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Modelled labour mobility and wage linkage do causal work; price data alone fit multiple mechanisms.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Regional political histories and data are uneven; colonial intervention, quality changes and capital constraints are not independently isolated by these prices.

**Inference limit:** Model assumptions include mobile labour, subsistence-linked pay and initially non-traded grain. This is an interpretation, not exogenous identification of drought or Mughal decline.

## IND-K15

Inikori argues that Atlantic demand linked to slavery-based commodity production helped form the markets and regional expansion within which British cotton mechanisation developed.

**Cases:** IND-C04; IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S13: Atlantic Slavery and the Rise of the Capitalist Global Economy](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.1086/709818), sections I–III, especially section II paragraphs on Lancashire’s wages and Atlantic export sales. Supports: Connects regional protoindustry, Atlantic demand and later mechanisation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Slavery-linked Atlantic commodity trade -> expanding markets -> regional protoindustry growth -> investment and mechanisation incentives.
- **strongest rival:** Domestic markets, factor prices or engineering can explain innovation; Atlantic demand might follow rather than precede industrial advantage.
- **discriminating observation:** Regional/sectoral demand exposure preceding investment, with comparable low-exposure producers and separate supply/finance channels.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S13 regional wage/exports sequence and IND-S11 competition account; temporal market linkage found, no isolated necessity test.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Atlantic markets, shipping, wages and political power are reciprocally connected; export shares are not investment causality.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Factor prices, domestic demand, engineering and other trading regions also matter; regional wage rankings do not by themselves adjudicate international wage comparisons.

**Inference limit:** An attributed historical mechanism, not a quantified necessary contribution of slavery to each invention. Export demand is not the same variable as investment finance.

## IND-K16

Inikori’s discussion of Charles Grant’s 1821 testimony identifies asymmetric British and Indian tariff power as a contemporary concern alongside improvements in machinery.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S13: Atlantic Slavery and the Rise of the Capitalist Global Economy](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.1086/709818), section IV, Charles Grant paragraph immediately preceding table 3. Supports: Reproduces an explicit contemporary argument about protection at home and political power over Indian imports.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial tariff authority -> asymmetric protection/import permission -> altered producer choice set and market access.
- **strongest rival:** Productivity and freight advantages alone would produce the same import shift under symmetric sovereignty.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable products under differing tariff regimes and original legislative/merchant records on excluded choices.
- **evidence sought found:** Read Grant testimony as reproduced in IND-S13 section IV; sought original-record specificity but did not acquire parliamentary source.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Testimony establishes awareness and constrained sovereignty more firmly than the counterfactual trade magnitude.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The testimony does not establish a tariff counterfactual for each cloth quality or exclude cost-driven competitive shifts.

**Inference limit:** Primary testimony is accessed through Inikori’s quotation via Chaudhuri; original parliamentary record not independently inspected. No causal magnitude assigned.

## IND-K17

Japanese mills in the 1890s adapted ring spinning through a package involving cotton imports, mixed fibres, more preparation labour and a changed labour force, rather than merely copying American operating conditions.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 549–551, table 2, and conclusion p. 561. Supports: Combines order specifications with historical accounts of cotton preparation and workforce change.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imported fibre variety plus labour-intensive mixing -> reduced breakage/broader fibre control -> viable rings under Japanese conditions.
- **strongest rival:** Changes in machine design or yarn demand alone could improve performance without preparation reorganisation.
- **discriminating observation:** Within comparable machinery and yarn counts, mixing adoption precedes improved throughput/breakage; costs and work hours measured.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S14 pp. 549–551, order table and contemporary observations: timing of mixed-cotton orders and package change support the pathway.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Order proxies do not independently identify labour, fibres and design; exact realised productivity not observed.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Cotton prices, yarn markets and suppliers changed concurrently; order specifications are not direct measurements of realised mill productivity.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish the separate effect of each component, or that the labour arrangements were freely chosen or beneficial to workers.

## IND-K18

Between 1878 and 1914 mule and ring spinning both continued to improve; Saxonhouse and Wright interpret British mule persistence as a choice supported by skills and product flexibility, not simply stagnant technology.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 537–542 and 552–554. Supports: Documents coexisting technologies, skill requirements and adoption models using changing technical frontiers.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Mule-specific skills and continuing machine improvement -> sustained capability -> continued purchases where advantageous.
- **strongest rival:** Entrenched employers/unions or sunk capital produce persistence despite inferior returns.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare realised quality-adjusted profits and best-practice improvements in new mule purchases versus feasible rings, not only old stocks.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S14 performance specifications and choice model: both technologies improve before 1914 and multiple countries buy mules.
- **remaining ambiguity:** New orders weaken a pure obsolete-stock story but may still reflect expectations, omitted costs or institutional constraints.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Rings later gained capabilities and mule performance growth slowed; rationality at one time does not demonstrate optimal long-run investment.

**Inference limit:** Technical specification proxies and nonexperimental firm-choice models do not isolate learning, and the authors’ strong claim about unavoidable British decline is not adopted here.

## IND-K19

India supplied the largest number of high-draft machinery orders in Saxonhouse and Wright’s 1929–1933 dataset, a bounded counterexample to equating low wages with non-adoption of labour-saving equipment.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 558–559, high-draft discussion. Supports: Reports destination ordering and qualifications to the labour-relations explanation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Order count is not an installed-stock share or necessarily a comparable fraction of each country’s capacity.

**Inference limit:** Restricted to the supplier dataset and interval; the paper’s proposed male-workforce/labour-relations mechanism remains tentative.

## IND-K20

In late Qing and Republican China, some handweavers combined machine-spun warp with hand-spun weft, and cheaper factory yarn also supported handloom weaving in new districts.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), pp. 373–374, notes 23–27. Supports: Describes mixed yarn use, Dingxian and geographical expansion of hand weaving.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cheap machine yarn -> replace selected spinning inputs -> handloom production retains product functions and expands accessible supply.
- **strongest rival:** General market growth alone may sustain hand weaving; poor households may simply be unable to buy cloth.
- **discriminating observation:** Yarn-price/access changes preceding weaving expansion, with product-level evidence of mixed warp/weft and comparison to inaccessible districts.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S15 pp. 373–374: explicit mixed yarn and named new weaving districts; original local studies not independently acquired.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Price, population, cash and fabric differences interact; mechanism stronger than national quantitative effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Hand spinning declined and factory cloth increased its share; hybrid persistence differed by region and product.

**Inference limit:** Earlier studies underlying Eyferth’s historical background were not independently acquired; no nationwide adoption rate is inferred from the examples.

## IND-K21

Eyferth reports that handloom cloth accounted for about 70 percent of China’s cotton textile output in 1934–1936, despite the expansion of factory production.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), p. 374, estimate and note 28. Supports: Provides the bounded period and output denominator for the reported estimate.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Handloom market share was declining and aggregation may mask regional variation; the underlying estimation method was not audited.

**Inference limit:** An attributed estimate from earlier research, not a newly verified census; cannot equate output share with employment share or household welfare.

## IND-K22

Eyferth documents moral and gendered classifications of textile work in late imperial and Republican China, including reform rhetoric that rendered household female production economically invisible.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), pp. 369–373, “Imagining a new national economy”. Supports: Contrasts gendered household ideals, diverse practice and later reformist definitions of productive work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Actual household divisions were more varied than prescriptive discourse; beliefs alone need not explain persistence when cash, prices and employment differ.

**Inference limit:** Documents classifications and interpretation, not freely expressed worker preferences. Post-1949 rationing mechanisms are excluded from this pre-1950 case.

## IND-K23

American factory electrification included distinct line-shaft, group-drive and unit-drive arrangements; replacing a prime mover with a motor did not automatically reorganise factory power distribution.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 353–363, figure 2 and line-shaft/group-drive sections. Supports: Documents the configurations, early retrofits and the functional differences.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Group drive remained useful in some applications, and older configurations survived beyond the main transition.

**Inference limit:** The engineering distinction is well documented; it supplies neither an exact date for universal replacement nor proof that all firms benefited from unit drive.

## IND-K24

Devine links effective unit-drive use to compatible machinery, production redesign, utility expansion and application services, with sunk onsite equipment differentiating retrofits from new plants.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 363, 368–371; machine redesign and Detroit Edison paragraphs. Supports: Describes indirect production benefits, compatibility constraints and services offered with electricity.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Compatible motors/tools plus redesign and service -> flexible local power -> productive reorganisation -> adoption benefits.
- **strongest rival:** Demand-led expansion or new capital generally causes both redesign and productivity, with motors only accompanying it.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched retrofits/new plants measuring workflow and output before/after, with comparable non-electric new equipment.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S16 configuration history, redesign comments and utility installation services; evidence for needed complements found, no clean experiment.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Selective engineering cases and firm growth can bias productivity interpretation; unit drive not always superior.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Firms varied in load, process and existing capital; some group-drive choices remained justified. Expanding firms may have selected electrification.

**Inference limit:** Not causal identification of all postwar productivity gains; worker pace, discretion and welfare are not assessed by the engineering evidence.

## IND-K25

Devine reports that electric motors represented roughly one-quarter of US manufacturing mechanical-drive horsepower in 1909 and over half in 1919; he separately infers unit-drive diffusion because comprehensive configuration statistics were unavailable.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 368–370, “Market penetration”. Supports: Provides the capacity denominator and explains indirect evidence for unit-drive timing.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Installed capacity is not utilisation, output, establishment adoption or the source of electricity generation.

**Inference limit:** Reuses the bounded historical series, not a reconstructed census; motor adoption must not be counted as complete factory redesign.

## LBR-K01

The AT&T historical study attributes delayed switching automation to organisational interdependencies and market-scale economics, rather than treating invention as immediate deployability.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [LBR-S01: Organizational and Economic Obstacles to Automation: A Cautionary Tale from AT&T in the Twentieth Century](https://pubsonline.informs.org/doi/10.1287/mnsc.2022.01760), Abstract and author-page summary. Supports: States adaptation and larger-market profitability mechanisms.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Interdependent manual service -> firm/technology adaptation -> viable switching at suitable scale -> staged adoption
- **strongest rival:** Purely technical improvement or wage-cost changes could drive timing without independent organisational causation.
- **discriminating observation:** Internal plans and comparable exchanges with different organisational readiness or market size.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S01 abstract reports archival interpretation; full mechanism evidence was not acquired, so no adjudication claimed.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The contribution cannot rank organisational adaptation versus technical and economic change.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Technology improvement and market growth were intertwined.

**Inference limit:** Abstract-level support; relative causal weights not audited.

## LBR-K02

In the telephone-automation study, incumbent operators and later young female cohorts experienced different labour-market outcomes; stable later-cohort employment did not mean incumbent workers were unharmed.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §VI, §VII and conclusion p.1934. Supports: Linked incumbent outcomes differ from repeated-cohort employment findings.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Dial cutover removes switching jobs -> incumbents lose specialised job opportunity -> adverse decade-later employment/occupational outcomes, while future entrants reallocate
- **strongest rival:** Different local demand trends or non-random linkage/selection could explain cutover timing and measured incumbent outcomes. Such trends should also appear in comparable local controls or pre-cutover outcomes; selection should be sensitive to linkage and weighting checks.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare linked incumbents with local matched controls separately from future cohorts around cutover timing; inspect pre-trends, alternative controls and linkage/weighting robustness. These checks constrain but do not eliminate unobserved selection.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S02 §§III,VI,VII inspected; study uses these distinct designs and reports different outcomes.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Unobserved local changes, linked-sample selection and incomplete wages remain possible limitations.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Genealogical linkage is non-random; the authors report robustness and weighting rather than eliminating every possible selection process.

**Inference limit:** Inference is conditional on comparison/selection assumptions; not a universal automation result.

## LBR-K03

The main later-cohort comparison focuses on white American-born women aged 16–25, while incumbent operators are followed through a separate linked census sample; neither sample directly represents all affected workers.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §III; Figures III/IV; Table VIII. Supports: Defines populations and separate linkage strategy.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The paper includes additional robustness populations, but the main population remains bounded.

**Inference limit:** Do not generalise to all women, racial groups, immigrants or displaced workers.

## LBR-K04

The authors find no clear overall wage effect for later young female cohorts, but the relevant tests cannot exclude wage changes around plus or minus 10 percent; no detected effect is not proof of unchanged pay.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §VII.B, pp.1927–1928. Supports: Explicitly discusses power and wide bounds in smaller wage-analysis sample.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Heterogeneous effects and measurement noise could mask changes.

**Inference limit:** Applies to these wage tests, not an exact confidence interval for every subgroup.

## LBR-K05

The roughly 60 percent estimate of 2018 US employment in job titles added since 1940 is an imputation from new-title shares within occupations, not a direct count of workers in new micro-titles.

**Cases:** LBR-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S03: New Frontiers: The Origins and Content of New Work, 1940–2018](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/2026-04/New-Frontiers-QJE-v139%283%29-p1399-1465-Aug-2024.pdf), §II.B, Figure I and accompanying pp.1410–1412. Supports: Explains the estimate and unavailable micro-title employment counts.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Index revisions and within-occupation title weighting affect measurement.

**Inference limit:** Do not restate as 60 percent of occupations invented since 1940, or as identified jobs caused by technology.

## LBR-K06

The new-work study links title emergence to both output-complementing innovation and demand shifts; its patent-based categories may overlap and cannot exhaust technological change.

**Cases:** LBR-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [LBR-S03: New Frontiers: The Origins and Content of New Work, 1940–2018](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/2026-04/New-Frontiers-QJE-v139%283%29-p1399-1465-Aug-2024.pdf), §II.C pp.1413–1419; Tables IV/V discussion. Supports: Defines exposure limitations and estimates augmentation/demand relationships.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Changed productive possibilities or market demand -> changed tasks/output -> new job specialties and employment allocation
- **strongest rival:** Relabelling and classification changes, or shared demand trends, could create apparent new work without substantive new tasks.
- **discriminating observation:** Check source-title consistency and compare innovation/demand exposures with placebo timing and alternative specifications.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S03 §II and Tables IV/V discuss construction, IV and placebo strategies; raw data not replicated.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Classification and identification assumptions remain; title addition does not directly measure worker entry costs or bargaining.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Automation and augmentation exposures are positively correlated; classification itself may contribute to overlap.

**Inference limit:** A US constructed-data study; neither title novelty nor patent similarity alone proves an accessible job opportunity.

## LBR-K07

A task-allocation model distinguishes labour displacement, productivity effects and creation of new labour tasks; positive productivity alone need not imply increased labour demand.

**Cases:** method/synthesis. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S04: Automation and New Tasks: How Technology Displaces and Reinstates Labor](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/publications/Automation%20and%20New%20Tasks%20-%20How%20Technology%20Displace.pdf), Introduction pp.3–5 and abstract. Supports: Sets out the model channels and their distinct implications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Aggregate demand, ownership, institutions and adjustment alter realised outcomes.

**Inference limit:** This is a model distinction, not an unconditional causal claim about every technology.

## MAT-K01

Xianrendong pottery-bearing contexts were dated to approximately 20,000–19,000 calibrated years BP, long before farming; this dates surviving use, not the invention of pottery.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S01: Early Pottery at 20,000 Years Ago in Xianrendong Cave, China](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/228089260_Early_Pottery_at_20000_Years_Ago_in_Xianrendong_Cave_China), pp. 1697–1699; Tables 1–2. Supports: Associated bone/charcoal dates and micromorphological checks of the deposits.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports contextual dating, not a causal explanation of adoption.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The dated materials are associated deposits; some strata were affected by small-scale bioturbation.

**Inference limit:** No first inventor, unique origin, or regional prevalence follows from this site.

## MAT-K02

Xianrendong fragments document vessel-making among foragers, while exterior burning makes cooking plausible rather than identifying particular foods.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S01: Early Pottery at 20,000 Years Ago in Xianrendong Cave, China](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/228089260_Early_Pottery_at_20000_Years_Ago_in_Xianrendong_Cave_China), pp. 1696–1697. Supports: Forager context, temper, forming methods, and burned exterior surfaces.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes vessel evidence and a qualified use inference, not why people adopted pottery.

**Counterevidence/rival:** No reconstructed complete vessels or direct residue identification in this study.

**Inference limit:** Multiple sherds do not establish routine household deployment or mass production.

## MAT-K03

The Japanese sample maintained a strong aquatic-processing association across the Pleistocene–Holocene transition, challenging a simple account in which pottery expanded principally to process newly abundant terrestrial foods.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S02: The impact of environmental change on the use of early pottery by East Asian hunter-gatherers](https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.1803782115), Results and Discussion; Table 1; Conclusion. Supports: 801 samples across 46 sites; conservative aquatic assignments of 73/156 and 256/566 lipid-bearing samples in the two periods.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Warming changes available foods -> users adopt/use more pots for new terrestrial processing -> pottery expands and contents diversify. The evidence tests the predicted functional change.
- **strongest rival:** Continued aquatic processing with changing fishing practices or social conventions also permits ceramic expansion.
- **discriminating observation:** Time-matched, preservation-controlled changes in plant/terrestrial versus aquatic residues, combined with independent food availability and vessel production measures.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S02 explicitly tested environmental/period expectations and found strong aquatic continuity; it did not identify every pot’s contents or measure all household use.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Poorly detected plants and lipid mixing remain possible; residue continuity cannot by itself identify a psychological cultural norm.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Plant foods and low-lipid contents are imperfectly detectable; functional continuity need not imply unchanged diet.

**Inference limit:** The sample concerns surviving sampled ceramics, not shares of all food or all households; motives remain interpretive.

## MAT-K04

In the surveyed southern Levant contexts, utilitarian iron appears in Iron I but becomes dominant among classified metal tools/weapons only in Iron IIA, while bronze manufacture continues.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 286–294; abstract; Iron Production during the Iron IIA. Supports: Contextual counts distinguish initial use from tenth–ninth-century BCE dominance and continuing bronze work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports chronology and assemblage composition rather than a cause.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Recovery, corrosion and recycling differ by metal and context.

**Inference limit:** Archaeological assemblage dominance is not a regional household adoption percentage.

## MAT-K05

The southern Levant evidence weakens an explanation that a general bronze-input shortage forced the transition: copper production continued and the authors report persistent tin-bronze compositions.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 298–299, Copper Production and Bronzeworking. Supports: Arabah copper chronology and 95 copper-alloy objects with reported average tin contents maintained around 5–6 wt%.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Bronze inputs become generally scarce -> producers/users substitute iron -> iron gains use. The claim tests the broad scarcity premise.
- **strongest rival:** Continued bronze supply plus changed iron production organisation can yield the same transition without general shortage.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated procurement interruptions or price/access deterioration preceding substitution, contrasted with areas retaining supply.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 pp. 298–299 reports copper-production continuity and maintained alloy tin levels; prices and distribution were not found, and the tin dataset is unpublished there.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Stable composition can coexist with scarcity, higher prices or selective allocation; geographically local shocks remain possible.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 95-object chemical dataset was unpublished; scarcity could affect prices or places without changing surviving alloy percentages.

**Inference limit:** Does not disprove every local or short-lived supply disruption; neither prices nor access distributions are observed.

## MAT-K06

The inspected Canaan study does not find a heat-treatment breakthrough that accounts for iron dominance; carbon content alone is insufficient evidence of deliberate superior steelmaking.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 295–297; p. 300. Supports: Metallography, corrosion limits, spontaneous carburisation, and absence of a clear chronological technical leap.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Improved heat treatment -> reliably superior iron objects -> users replace bronze. The claim tests a chronological breakthrough.
- **strongest rival:** More reliable/available iron supply or institutional demand can expand use without a new heat-treatment threshold.
- **discriminating observation:** A well-dated step change in treatment and task-specific performance preceding adoption, with comparable preservation and alloy alternatives.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 pp. 295–300 reports no clear technical leap in sampled material and explains carbon/corrosion ambiguities.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Corrosion, unpublished detail and missing exceptional workshops may hide changes; hardness alone is not complete performance.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Preservation restricts metallography; exceptional objects may escape the sample.

**Inference limit:** Failure to detect a breakthrough does not prove equal cost, durability, repairability or battlefield performance.

## MAT-K07

Iron work associated with older bronze-working areas and public architecture makes local craft adaptation and administrative support plausible contributors to systematic production.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 291–293, 299–300. Supports: Spatial/chronological association of metalworking and evidence for urban smelting or forging.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Existing metal skills plus administrative support -> local smelting/forging capacity -> sustained iron production and wider use.
- **strongest rival:** Co-location may reflect ordinary urban facilities or market access, while iron demand draws administrations to existing work.
- **discriminating observation:** Sequenced production investment, inventories, institutional orders or controlled access preceding expansion; comparison with independent workshops.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 documents metalworking continuity and public-architecture association, but no contracts or direct ownership evidence.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reverse causation and shared urban location fit the record; early production remains are sparse.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Iron I production remains are lacking; imported iron or mobile specialists remain possible.

**Inference limit:** Workshop location alone neither identifies ownership nor demonstrates compulsion, funding or a state monopoly.

## MAT-K08

Chinese iron history requires separate chronologies for meteoritic objects, bloomery production, casting and practical cast-iron implements; later finds revised earlier accounts of a wholly southern, cast-iron beginning.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S04: The earliest use of iron in China](https://wuecampus.uni-wuerzburg.de/moodle/pluginfile.php/4671985/mod_resource/content/1/Wagner%2C%20Donald%20B.%20The%20earliest%20use%20of%20iron%20in%20China.pdf), sections 1, 3.4 and 4. Supports: Explicit revision of the author’s previous reconstruction.
- [MAT-S15: Questioning Diversity (of Iron) in the Workplace: Bloomery Iron, Cast Iron, China and the West](https://intarch.ac.uk/journal/issue69/14/full-text.html), Table 1; sections 2–3. Supports: Differentiates first appearance, production and functional use, with disputed dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes distinctions in evidence and changes in scholarship; no transmission mechanism asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Early object dating and production identification remain contested; surviving objects do not locate first smelters.

**Inference limit:** Supports branching stages, not a definite single transmission route or inventor.

## MAT-K09

Chinese cast-iron production plausibly combined iron smelting with bronze-casting and ceramic/kiln expertise, but the claim that political suppression explains the absence of alternative iron routes remains a hypothesis.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S15: Questioning Diversity (of Iron) in the Workplace: Bloomery Iron, Cast Iron, China and the West](https://intarch.ac.uk/journal/issue69/14/full-text.html), sections on cast-iron annealing and Access to technology; concluding discussion. Supports: Proposes cross-craft integration and discusses moulds, kilns, fuel, labour and state power.
- [MAT-S04: The earliest use of iron in China](https://wuecampus.uni-wuerzburg.de/moodle/pluginfile.php/4671985/mod_resource/content/1/Wagner%2C%20Donald%20B.%20The%20earliest%20use%20of%20iron%20in%20China.pdf), section 4. Supports: Earlier hypothesis connecting agricultural implement forms with iron casting.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Links among smelting, casting and ceramic expertise -> controlled casting/treatment -> practical cast-iron output; state support may organise this bundle.
- **strongest rival:** Local ore/task economics and independent craft exchange could create the same bundle without coercive political suppression.
- **discriminating observation:** Securely phased co-located processes and independent evidence of organisation; competing production under/away from claimed restrictions.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S15 proposes the bundle and stronger political account; MAT-S04 documents revised early histories. No enforcement archive was inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Facilities identify technical contributions more securely than ownership, suppression or counterfactual western failure.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 2025 comparison aggregates diverse regions and infers institutions partly from outcomes.

**Inference limit:** No comparative cost accounts or direct policy enforcement evidence here identify the causal contribution of suppression.

## MAT-K10

Song–Yuan mineral fuel in iron manufacture is supported by texts and anomalously old carbon dates in castings, but a universal shift to coke, or one exact production route, is not established.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S05: Blast Furnaces in Song–Yuan China](https://donwagner.dk/arch-iron/resources/Wagner2001.pdf), pp. 51–53 and 58–61. Supports: Su Shi text, sulphur evidence, radiocarbon results, and distinction between smelting and remelting.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes support for mineral-fuel use and its observational limits.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Charcoal remained possible in other furnaces; carbon can enter during remelting as well as smelting.

**Inference limit:** Neither national adoption share nor coal/coke ratio follows from the few dated objects.

## MAT-K11

The often-repeated large estimate of Song iron output is not a secure observation: Wagner explicitly rejects the reliability of the extrapolation from state quota figures.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S05: Blast Furnaces in Song–Yuan China](https://donwagner.dk/arch-iron/resources/Wagner2001.pdf), p. 42 n. 6. Supports: Discusses the roughly 115,000 metric tonne estimate and its flawed underlying assumptions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports an explicit methodological criticism of an estimate.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The author considers the magnitude plausible; criticism does not establish low output.

**Inference limit:** Do not use disputed output as a denominator for productivity or an adoption rate.

## MAT-K12

Meroe replica smelts improved after changes in ore, air delivery and operation, demonstrating coupled operating constraints rather than the sufficiency of a copied furnace shape.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S06: Iron Smelting in Sudan: Experimental Archaeology at The Royal City of Meroe](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/58594bce-8624-430b-822e-bdec34bf76e6/content), Tables 2–4; Lessons Learned, pp. 412–415. Supports: Failed/low-yield trials, changes to ores and bellows, viable later operation and explicit experimental limitations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Suitable ore plus adjusted blast/charging and operator coordination -> better reduction/slag behaviour -> more workable products in replica trials.
- **strongest rival:** Different modern operating choices could also succeed; apparent improvement could result from one unisolated variable rather than the whole historical reconstruction.
- **discriminating observation:** Controlled replication changing one parameter at a time, matching archaeological residues and retaining detailed mass balances.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S06 Tables 2–4 and Lessons Learned record failed/low-yield runs and improved combinations; authors flag anecdotal later data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Joint changes prevent attribution to each input; viability is demonstrated only for tested modern configurations, not a unique ancient procedure.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Parameters changed together; some later successes lacked detailed records.

**Inference limit:** Modern reconstructed feasibility does not identify the unique ancient process or provide an ancient output series.

## MAT-K13

Charcoal analysis at Meroe and Hamadab shows persistent, selective use of Acacia type nilotica in metallurgical contexts across a long production history.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S07: Fuel selection during long-term ancient iron production in Sudan](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/332221733_Fuel_selection_during_long-term_ancient_iron_production_in_Sudan), Tables 1–2; pp. 34–39 and 45–47. Supports: 1,754 analysed charcoal fragments overall; strong metallurgical dominance contrasted with limited non-metallurgical assemblages.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes observed charcoal selection over dated contexts.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Comparison contexts are small and not always contemporaneous; taxonomic identification sometimes tentative.

**Inference limit:** Persistent fuel selection does not by itself prove woodland management, local sourcing or unchanged production intensity.

## MAT-K14

Meroe’s charcoal assemblages do not establish smelting-induced deforestation; stable fuel access is compatible with management, lower intensity, efficient operation or imports.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S07: Fuel selection during long-term ancient iron production in Sudan](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/332221733_Fuel_selection_during_long-term_ancient_iron_production_in_Sudan), pp. 47–50, especially Conclusions. Supports: Authors explicitly enumerate alternative supply explanations and unknown exchange range.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sustained iron demand -> heavy local wood removal -> depletion/changed fuel use or production decline; charcoal should register some consequences if local sourcing persists.
- **strongest rival:** Managed regeneration, smaller output, efficient operation or fuel imports maintain selected charcoal despite different local ecological conditions.
- **discriminating observation:** Independent vegetation history and charcoal provenance/procurement radius alongside dated production intensity.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S07 finds persistent selection and explicitly enumerates alternative supply explanations, with no demonstrated shortage signal.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Imports or expanding catchments can hide local degradation; charcoal selectivity is not a woodland census.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Ecological degradation could coexist with continued access through a widening collection catchment.

**Inference limit:** No landscape-scale vegetation reconstruction or procurement-cost series is supplied; absence of a charcoal signal is not proof of ecological harmlessness.

## MAT-K15

Controlled comparisons show that tin bronze’s greater possible hardness does not make it universally superior to arsenical bronze; ductility and sheet-forming performance can favour the latter.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 500–508; Tables 4–6; Figs. 25–27. Supports: Mechanical tests and comparison with thin-sheet fabrication traditions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Alloy composition and working treatment -> different hardness/ductility/toughness -> different suitability for specified sheet-forming tasks.
- **strongest rival:** A universal tin-bronze superiority account predicts better performance across relevant tasks, rather than a trade-off.
- **discriminating observation:** Controlled processing of both alloys followed by relevant mechanical tests and task failure assessment.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S08 directly tests alloys and identifies different advantages; pp. 500–508 discuss limits of elongation data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** This tests material behaviour, not whether historical smiths selected it for the inferred reason or whether costs favoured it.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some tests need more reproducible elongation measurements; alloy concentration and processing strongly affect results.

**Inference limit:** Modern mechanical advantage is not a complete explanation of archaeological selection or user value.

## MAT-K16

Experimental cosmelting of Peruvian copper oxides with arsenic-bearing sulphide ores produced usable copper–arsenic alloys across multiple mixtures, supplying a feasible alternative to a separate roasting sequence.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S09: The Production of Copper–Arsenic Alloys (Arsenic Bronze) by Cosmelting: Modern Experiment, Ancient Practice](https://www.scribd.com/document/374679205/Lechtman-H-The-Production-of-Copper-Arsenic-Alloys-by-Co-Smelting), pp. 497–499, 523–526; Table 11. Supports: Crucible/furnace tests without pre-roasting or added flux and discussion of naturally mixed versus deliberately mixed ores.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes achieved modern experimental feasibility, not a causal historical adoption account.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Feasible experiments do not show which ancient workshops used this process or how often.

**Inference limit:** No universal origin sequence, automatic intention, ancient occupational-exposure level or cost advantage can be inferred.

## MAT-K17

The Andean history summarized by Lechtman includes continued arsenical-bronze use alongside Inka tin bronze, making coexistence and political distribution more appropriate than a complete superior-alloy replacement story.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 477–479. Supports: Regional ore geography, coexistence and interpretation of Inka alloy distribution.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial access/control over tin and distribution -> supply of tin bronze across political space -> partial substitution with local continuities.
- **strongest rival:** Ore geography, transport costs and task performance can generate regional compositions without imperial standardisation as the primary cause.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable sites before/after incorporation, official versus local products, and independently established control of mines/flows.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S08 pp. 477–479 reports coexistence and interprets political distribution; underlying local-control studies not independently inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Political and resource effects likely interact; no identified causal effect or complete displacement.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Underlying mine-control and closure evidence was not independently audited in this contribution.

**Inference limit:** Political explanation is plausible but not identified against transport costs, ore supply and task-specific demand.

## MAT-K18

Hosler’s West Mexican studies describe tools as well as objects whose sound and colour mattered, so technological performance cannot be reduced to cutting hardness or military lethality.

**Cases:** MAT-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S10: Sound, color and meaning in the metallurgy of Ancient West Mexico](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/00438243.1995.9980295), abstract. Supports: Distinguishes tools/status items and relates bells and metallic colours to ritual and war symbolism.
- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 506–508. Supports: Mechanical/physical properties and American object selection.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Particular sonic/visual values -> patrons/users select bells and colours -> smiths develop relevant material properties.
- **strongest rival:** Functional availability, general display demand or analyst selection may produce similar assemblages without shared specific beliefs.
- **discriminating observation:** Object-level material choices combined with independently contextualised historical/linguistic evidence and non-elite comparisons.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S10 abstract states laboratory and ethnohistoric interpretation; MAT-S08 discusses American physical properties. Full symbolic argument unavailable.
- **remaining ambiguity:** No independently audited belief evidence here; not every consumer or object need share the proposed meanings.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 1995 full text was not acquired; frequency and direct historical passages have not been audited.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish that every object was ritual, that all consumers shared values, or that productive utility was absent.

## MAT-K19

Hosler proposed South/Central American transmission into West Mexico combined with local technical transformation; this is a claim about selective recombination, not passive copying.

**Cases:** MAT-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S11: Ancient West Mexican Metallurgy: South and Central American Origins and West Mexican Transformations](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1525/aa.1988.90.4.02a00040), abstract. Supports: Two proposed phases of technical links and explicit local reinterpretation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Interregional contact -> movement of know-how/components -> local recombination -> distinctive West Mexican production.
- **strongest rival:** Convergence from similar resources/tasks can create similarities without the proposed transfer; object movement alone may not transmit skills.
- **discriminating observation:** Diagnostic production sequences, intermediate chronology, source/provenance and contact evidence evaluated together.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S11 abstract proposes two transmission phases and transformations; detailed supporting catalogue unavailable.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Neither routes, carriers nor first dates independently verified; similarity alone fits several accounts.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Only abstract read; dates differ between the 1988 and 1995 formulations. Similarity alone cannot exclude convergence.

**Inference limit:** Transfer route and carriers remain unverified here; neither isolation nor a complete imported package is assumed.

## MAT-K20

Chemical study of 52 Igbo Olokun beads supports locally distinctive glass groups; related beads elsewhere support exchange, while imported glass at other Ile-Ife sites rules out a wholly isolated setting.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S13: Chemical analysis of glass beads from Igbo Olokun, Ile-Ife (SW Nigeria): New light on raw materials, production, and interregional interactions](https://www.academia.edu/35714341/Chemical_analysis_of_glass_beads_from_Igbo_Olokun_Ile_Ife_SW_Nigeria_New_light_on_raw_materials_production_and_interregional_interactions_Babalola_et_al_2018_JAS_90_), sections 2, 3.4, 4.1–4.3 and 5; Table 3. Supports: Selected-sample chemistry, local raw-material compatibility, regional distribution and other-site soda-lime glass.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Local glass production -> beads circulate through exchange -> related chemical groups occur elsewhere.
- **strongest rival:** Multiple makers using similar raw materials or redistribution from an unlocated production centre could share chemical signatures.
- **discriminating observation:** Distinctive trace patterns tied to production debris and secure regional chronology; tests of other potential sources.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S13 reports local bead chemistry, production debris and regional matches, with imported glass in other city contexts.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Local production is well supported, but origin of every distant bead and direction/organisation of exchange are not uniquely identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** 52 beads are a small selected fraction; deposits disturbed; compatible raw materials are not a uniquely sourced quarry.

**Inference limit:** No import share for the entire city, market size, annual output or proven transmission of production skills follows.

## MAT-K21

Ile-Ife glass crucibles were specialised ceramic complements; reported usable capacities are evidence of batch capability, not a measurement of sustained industrial throughput.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S12: The Glass Making Crucibles from Ile-Ife, SW Nigeria](https://repository.cyi.ac.cy/bitstream/CyI/1939/1/Babalola%20et%20al%202018%20Glassmaking%20crucibles%20Ile-Ife%20JAA%2016.pdf), pp. 31–32, abstract. Supports: Ten analysed samples, refractory clay, estimated 1–7 L usable capacities.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes reported crucible capacities and measurement scope.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Body tables not acquired; vessel reconstruction and actual fill levels carry uncertainty.

**Inference limit:** Do not multiply capacity by an invented number of firings, workshops or years.

## MAT-K22

Incompletely reacted glass batch on Ile-Ife crucibles directly strengthens the case for local primary glassmaking, while recipe uniqueness alone cannot establish an origin independent of all external ideas.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S14: Semi-finished glass from Ile-Ife, Nigeria: implications for the archaeology of glass in sub-Saharan Africa](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10104101/3/Rehren_Babalola%20et%20al%20Antiquity%202020%20final%20version.pdf), accepted manuscript pp. 2–4, Laboratory analysis and Implications. Supports: Residual quartz/feldspar, new reaction phases and heterogeneous glass distinguish raw-batch melting from only remelting finished imports.
- [MAT-S13: Chemical analysis of glass beads from Igbo Olokun, Ile-Ife (SW Nigeria): New light on raw materials, production, and interregional interactions](https://www.academia.edu/35714341/Chemical_analysis_of_glass_beads_from_Igbo_Olokun_Ile_Ife_SW_Nigeria_New_light_on_raw_materials_production_and_interregional_interactions_Babalola_et_al_2018_JAS_90_), sections 4.2–4.3. Supports: Locally compatible recipe and participation in exchange.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Identifies material evidence of a production operation; does not claim an adoption cause or independent-invention mechanism.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Museum-store fragments lack newly excavated sealed contexts; authors advance a stronger autonomous-origin interpretation.

**Inference limit:** Local primary production is better established than the exact invention date, transmission history or subsequent disappearance.

## MAT-K23

Recycling and composite manufacture complicate inferences from surviving iron objects to initial production, replacement rates or material scarcity.

**Cases:** MAT-C02; MAT-C03. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S16: The sword that was broken …: The detection of recycled iron in the archaeological record](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/arcm.12892), Introduction; Iron production and the forms of iron recycling; Conclusions. Supports: Detection problems and selected examples of reused iron; distinction between manufacture and reuse.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Reuse/composite fabrication -> multiple material histories within surviving objects -> object count/chemistry does not uniquely identify new production.
- **strongest rival:** New composite manufacture without old scrap can produce some similar structures; differential preservation also changes counts.
- **discriminating observation:** Diagnostic reused fragments or incompatible material histories, compared with production structures of known new composites.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S16 supplies selected examples and explicitly warns that common manufacturing features can mimic recycling.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Methodological non-identifiability is supported; no recycling rate or actual local pathway is measured for MAT-C02/C03.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some structural evidence is consistent with new composite manufacture as well as recycling.

**Inference limit:** A methodological warning, not a recycling percentage for either case; selective later parallels are not proof of earlier local practice.

## MAT-K24

Across these purposively selected cases, repeatable processes, complementary crafts and task-specific demand are better-supported comparison variables than a universal ranking of materials.

**Cases:** MAT-C01; MAT-C02; MAT-C03; MAT-C04; MAT-C05; MAT-C06; MAT-C07. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S02: The impact of environmental change on the use of early pottery by East Asian hunter-gatherers](https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.1803782115), Results and Conclusion. Supports: Functional persistence despite changes in availability.
- [MAT-S06: Iron Smelting in Sudan: Experimental Archaeology at The Royal City of Meroe](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/58594bce-8624-430b-822e-bdec34bf76e6/content), Lessons Learned and Conclusion. Supports: Coupled operating variables.
- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 500–508. Supports: Task-dependent alloy properties.
- [MAT-S14: Semi-finished glass from Ile-Ife, Nigeria: implications for the archaeology of glass in sub-Saharan Africa](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10104101/3/Rehren_Babalola%20et%20al%20Antiquity%202020%20final%20version.pdf), Laboratory analysis. Supports: Local raw-material transformation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Task requirements and accessible complementary operations -> workable production/adoption choices -> branching and coexisting technologies.
- **strongest rival:** Different episodes may instead be dominated by independent resource shocks or political power; purposive selection may overstate recurrence.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched task/region contrasts with failed production, available alternatives and independent measures of demand/institutions.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S02, S06, S08 and S14 supply different kinds of bounded evidence; they do not constitute a representative comparative design.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Cross-case recurrence is not causal identification; outcomes and source survival affect selection.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Selection emphasises contrasting outcomes and cannot estimate how frequently any mechanism dominated.

**Inference limit:** A research framework rather than causal identification, universal law or forecast.

## MED-K01

Early eighteenth-century reports connect British variolation discussions to Ottoman practitioners, including women, and African expertise conveyed through Onesimus; this is evidence of transmission rather than a British origin.

**Cases:** MED-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S01: The origins of inoculation](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1258/jrsm.2012.12k044), Inoculation in parts of the Ottoman Empire and Europe. Supports: Reproduces the relevant reports and correspondence.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about documented knowledge channels, not their causal share in uptake.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Surviving elite intermediaries may obscure earlier local transmission.

**Inference limit:** No continental prevalence, precise birthplace or unique transmission route is inferred.

## MED-K02

The inspected variolation evidence does not establish a unique origin or secure ancient chronology.

**Cases:** MED-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S01: The origins of inoculation](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1258/jrsm.2012.12k044), Inoculation in China and India; Conclusions. Supports: Contradictory testimony, different methods and uncertain early dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sparse surviving accounts -> reconstruction of routes -> uncertain origin attribution
- **strongest rival:** Independent development or earlier undocumented diffusion also fits surviving accounts
- **discriminating observation:** Contemporaneous dated descriptions with identifiable technique and transmission contacts
