- **evidence sought found:** MED-S01 checked early-origin claims against its source criticism; found uncertainty and conjecture
- **remaining ambiguity:** Original local-language records not independently inspected.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Boylston proposes a common route, but labels parts speculative.

**Inference limit:** Neither silence in surviving texts nor similarity of methods proves absence or common descent.

## MED-K03

Ottoman vaccination policy combined free provision commitments, multilingual instruction and religious endorsement before and alongside later compulsory rules.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §2 Normalizing and facilitating vaccination. Supports: 1840 free-provision decree, 1845 endorsement and 1846 multilingual treatise.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes documented policy instruments, not their effectiveness.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Policy texts describe intended provision, not universal performance.

**Inference limit:** No effect size for price, education or religious messaging is identified.

## MED-K04

Ottoman universal-vaccination ambitions were constrained by vaccine production, personnel and rural delivery capacity; legal scope exceeded documented implementation.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §§2–5. Supports: Contrasts legislative expansion with production and staffing limitations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Wide mandate plus limited supplies/staff -> inability to deliver repeatedly -> incomplete coverage
- **strongest rival:** Adequate provision with refusal could also yield low coverage
- **discriminating observation:** Local records of stock availability, staff visits and refusal under the same rule
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S02 explicitly contrasts mandate with resource limits and easier institutional reach; no comparable district data supplied
- **remaining ambiguity:** Capacity constraints supported, relative contribution against distrust unmeasured.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Fear and refusal, instability and war could also depress uptake.

**Inference limit:** No district panel separates supply, trust and coercion effects.

## MED-K05

The 1885 Ottoman rules conditioned school entry and state-linked roles on vaccination evidence; later rules enlarged obligations and penalties, so legally induced uptake cannot be equated with consent.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §3 1885 and 1894 regulations; §4 1903 and 1915 expansions. Supports: Specific conditions, fines and certification rules.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Documents compulsion instruments and the logical limit of inferring consent from compliance.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The same source reports incomplete enforcement and implementation.

**Inference limit:** Legal exposure is not evidence of actual penalties or every household's inability to evade.

## MED-K06

Lister’s 1867 address presented an evolving wound-treatment practice through cases and claimed ward improvements, rather than a controlled trial of a fixed protocol.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S03: On the Antiseptic Principle in the Practice of Surgery (1867; 2010 reprint)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2895849/), Opening clinical discussion; paragraph beginning If the severest forms; concluding ward discussion. Supports: Procedural adaptations, case reports and asserted outcomes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Characterizes the evidence form and treatment specification in the historical document.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Observations can be informative despite lacking controlled identification.

**Inference limit:** Does not deny antisepsis efficacy or validate every claim in Lister's address.

## MED-K07

Contemporary objections to Lister’s aggregate outcome comparisons included small samples, case comparability and hospital epidemic cycles, making simple before/after attribution contestable.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S05: Statistics and the British controversy about the effects of Joseph Lister’s system of antisepsis for surgery, 1867–1890](https://www.jameslindlibrary.org/articles/statistics-and-the-british-controversy-about-the-effects-of-joseph-listers-system-of-antisepsis-for-surgery-1867-1890/), Statistics; Glasgow Royal Infirmary; Longer-term perspective. Supports: Examines selection periods, small numbers and changing background mortality.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Chosen comparison periods -> apparent mortality reduction -> efficacy inference
- **strongest rival:** Epidemic cycles, selection and case mix can produce a similar comparison
- **discriminating observation:** Longer ward series, comparable operations and independent comparison wards
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S05 reports wide prior fluctuation and very small operation counts, weakening the simple inference
- **remaining ambiguity:** Does not separate all genuine treatment effects from confounding; critiques are not proof of no benefit.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Improvement in fracture management and other outcomes may still support antiseptic benefits.

**Inference limit:** Hamilton reconstruction is mediated through Tröhler; no independent raw-data reanalysis.

## MED-K08

Demonstration, detailed instruction and embodied operative skills contributed to communicating Listerian practice; adoption was not reducible to assent to germ theory.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S04: Joseph Lister and the performance of antiseptic surgery](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3744349/), Antiseptic performance; Professional performance. Supports: Traces demonstrations, hands-on details, students and adaptation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** New multi-step regimen -> demonstration and practice -> ability to reproduce treatment
- **strongest rival:** Prestige and self-selection of adherents may explain following the same teachers
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable surgeons with and without direct training, measured performance rather than declared allegiance
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S04 documents channels and adaptations but does not supply such matched outcomes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Contribution is plausible and documented; necessity or magnitude not identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Reputation, materials and institutional differences could explain the same diffusion pattern.

**Inference limit:** No controlled comparison isolates training from selection of motivated trainees.

## MED-K09

Alsan and Goldin estimate that combined safe water and effective sewerage account for about one-third of the decline in log under-five mortality in their jointly treated Massachusetts municipalities, 1880–1920; the interventions are complementary in their preferred model.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MED-S06: Watersheds in Child Mortality: The Role of Effective Water and Sewerage Infrastructure, 1880–1920](https://bpb-us-e1.wpmucdn.com/sites.harvard.edu/dist/e/935/files/2025/11/alsangoldin_jpe.pdf), pp. 589–590; identification discussion and robustness. Supports: Staggered completion design and reported decomposition of 26.6 out of 79.2 log points.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Combined exposure-route interventions -> reduced pathogen exposure -> lower child mortality
- **strongest rival:** Other changes or selective migration timed with construction could mimic the effect
- **discriminating observation:** No pre-trend divergence, age/cause specificity and stable composition around completion
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S06 reports pre-trend checks, age/cause contrasts, Table 4 composition checks and robustness
- **remaining ambiguity:** Observational identification remains conditional; engineering rollout not random assignment.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Other sanitary improvements, spillovers and population selection could threaten the design.

**Inference limit:** Local estimate under model assumptions; neither a universal effect nor the national mortality contribution.

## MED-K10

Regional compulsory participation and payment formed part of the Massachusetts sewerage response to pollution crossing municipal boundaries.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S06: Watersheds in Child Mortality: The Role of Effective Water and Sewerage Infrastructure, 1880–1920](https://bpb-us-e1.wpmucdn.com/sites.harvard.edu/dist/e/935/files/2025/11/alsangoldin_jpe.pdf), p. 589 and §II.B pp. 592–593. Supports: Upstream/downstream problem and legally coordinated regional system.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cross-boundary sewage costs -> regional obligations -> coordinated investment
- **strongest rival:** Political influence or voluntary coordination could deliver similar investment
- **discriminating observation:** Evidence of failed voluntary arrangements and subsequent contribution changes under mandate
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S06 documents boundary problem and mandate; not a counterfactual comparison of governance regimes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Observed institutional route does not prove necessity.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Voluntary bargains or private coordination might have supplied a viable alternative.

**Inference limit:** No claim that compulsion was uniquely necessary, consensual or costlessly beneficial.

## MED-K11

The size of clean-water contributions to aggregate US mortality decline remains disputed across analyses using overlapping records and different coding choices.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S07: Re-Examining the Contribution of Public Health Efforts to the Decline in Urban Mortality (July 2019 manuscript)](https://www.aeaweb.org/conference/2020/preliminary/paper/s855Kd6r), §3 pp. 8–9; §6 pp. 17–18; conclusion. Supports: Smaller estimates and sensitivity to transcription, dates and denominators.
- [MED-S08: Reexamining the Contribution of Public Health Efforts to the Decline in Urban Mortality: Comment](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/app.20190711), Abstract. Supports: Defends substantial total effect while acknowledging reduced infant estimate and coding differences.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Record coding and intervention timing -> different panel estimates -> different historical attribution
- **strongest rival:** Real treatment heterogeneity across cities could also explain differences
- **discriminating observation:** Reestimate with harmonized raw counts, population and partial-year rules
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S07 reports within-data corrections; MED-S08 attributes remaining difference to coding and denominators
- **remaining ambiguity:** No independent replication here; shared data means source count is not independent corroboration.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The disagreement does not erase agreement that filtration reduced typhoid mortality.

**Inference limit:** Final-paper data and reply not audited; MED-S08 abstract cannot adjudicate calculations.

## MED-K12

Wartime penicillin supply required solving culture-yield and downstream-processing problems in addition to establishing clinical promise.

**Cases:** MED-C05. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S09: Penicillin Production through Deep-tank Fermentation](https://www.acs.org/education/whatischemistry/landmarks/penicillin.html), Penicillin Production by Deep-tank Fermentation. Supports: Scale-up, extraction, purification and sterile production.
- [MED-S10: The Rescue of Penicillin](https://www.ars.usda.gov/oc/timeline/penicillin), 1941–1942 collaboration paragraphs. Supports: Culture medium and strain work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Low usable yield -> strain/media/process work -> more recoverable output
- **strongest rival:** Expansion of vessel numbers or different extraction routes might explain part of output growth
- **discriminating observation:** Measured yields and losses before/after controlled process changes
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S09/S10 describe process improvements and production transition; no primary batch logs inspected
- **remaining ambiguity:** Causal weights and alternative industrial routes remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Institutional accounts may overstate their own component's contribution.

**Inference limit:** Process-specific contribution, not proof that this exact route was the only possible route.

## MED-K13

Pfizer’s prior submerged-fermentation experience was an identified contributor to its penicillin production route.

**Cases:** MED-C05. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S09: Penicillin Production through Deep-tank Fermentation](https://www.acs.org/education/whatischemistry/landmarks/penicillin.html), Development of Deep-tank Fermentation. Supports: Connects gluconic-acid process knowledge to penicillin tanks.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Prior fermentation know-how -> adaptation of tanks/sterility controls -> production scale-up
- **strongest rival:** Capital and purchasing incentives could account for success despite different prior experience
- **discriminating observation:** Compare firms with similar finance and different inherited fermentation capabilities
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S09 names a concrete technical transfer; no matched firm histories examined
- **remaining ambiguity:** Source may organize history retrospectively around eventual success.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Capital, procurement access and new staff might matter as much as inherited skills.

**Inference limit:** Company-centred retrospective; not a cross-firm estimate or universal prerequisite.

## MED-K14

Governmental coordination and firms’ investment choices accompanied penicillin scale-up, while committing plant capacity involved opportunity costs.

**Cases:** MED-C05. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S09: Penicillin Production through Deep-tank Fermentation](https://www.acs.org/education/whatischemistry/landmarks/penicillin.html), Opening; Penicillin Production by Deep-tank Fermentation. Supports: Government approach to firms and risk of curtailing other products.
- [MED-S10: The Rescue of Penicillin](https://www.ars.usda.gov/oc/timeline/penicillin), Collaboration and four-company paragraphs. Supports: Public laboratory/industrial connection.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Wartime demand and public coordination -> commitments to risky capacity -> enlarged supply
- **strongest rival:** Private expected profitability could independently cause the investment
- **discriminating observation:** Dated contracts, capacity plans and internal investment deliberations
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S09/S10 document collaboration and resource commitment, not the counterfactual financing path
- **remaining ambiguity:** Correlation with wartime effort does not establish unique necessity.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Rising private expectations of demand might have induced investment without equivalent coordination.

**Inference limit:** No procurement-contract audit or cost-benefit estimate.

## MED-K15

Sri Lankan records reported six indigenous and eleven imported malaria cases in 1963, followed by reduced control activity and a major late-1960s resurgence.

**Cases:** MED-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S11: Analysis of Historical Trends and Recent Elimination of Malaria from Sri Lanka and Its Applicability for Malaria Control in Other Countries](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/public-health/articles/10.3389/fpubh.2017.00212/full), Malaria in Sri Lanka During the Second Half of the Twentieth Century. Supports: Counts and programme sequence.
- [MED-S12: Malaria resurgence: a systematic review and assessment of its causes](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/1475-2875-11-122), Weakening of the malaria control programme, Sri Lanka paragraph. Supports: Same sequence with surveillance and population-movement qualifications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Records temporal pattern and reported classifications without allocating causal shares.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Both accounts inherit overlapping historical records.

**Inference limit:** Case counts depend on detection; sequence alone is not a causal estimate.

## MED-K16

The inspected chronology does not justify attributing Sri Lanka’s initial late-1960s reversal solely to DDT resistance: relaxation began earlier, while the account dates emerging resistance to 1969. The account reports replacement of DDT by malathion in 1977.

**Cases:** MED-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S11: Analysis of Historical Trends and Recent Elimination of Malaria from Sri Lanka and Its Applicability for Malaria Control in Other Countries](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/public-health/articles/10.3389/fpubh.2017.00212/full), Second Half of the Twentieth Century. Supports: 1964 consolidation, later resurgence, 1969 resistance and 1977 replacement.
- [MED-S12: Malaria resurgence: a systematic review and assessment of its causes](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/1475-2875-11-122), Sri Lanka example and Technical problems. Supports: Multiple operational and ecological causes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Operational relaxation before reported resistance -> reduced suppression -> renewed transmission
- **strongest rival:** Undetected earlier resistance could also contribute
- **discriminating observation:** Contemporaneous susceptibility assays and local coverage preceding resurgence
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S11 chronology and MED-S12 multi-factor narrative inspected; first causal claim fails temporal sufficiency
- **remaining ambiguity:** Detection lag and intertwined ecological changes remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Resistance may have been present before detection and can interact with weakened operations.

**Inference limit:** Rejects a sole-cause assertion; does not establish absence of earlier resistance or a unique alternative cause.

## MED-K17

WHO announced Sri Lanka’s malaria-free certification in September 2016 after three consecutive years of documented zero transmission.

**Cases:** MED-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S16: Address by Regional Director on the occasion of Malaria Elimination Certification by the Government of Sri Lanka](https://www.who.int/southeastasia/news/speeches/detail/address-by-regional-director-on-the-occasion-of-malaria-elimination-certification-by-the-government-of-sri-lanka), Paragraph beginning On September 5, 2016. Supports: Dated public certification announcement and qualification period.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Dated official event with precisely bounded meaning.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Certification relies on programme surveillance and is not proof of zero imported infections.

**Inference limit:** Historical certification only; no statement about present-day status.

## MED-K18

Cohen et al. catalogued 75 malaria resurgence events; causes were reported attributions, and only 16% of suggested causes had substantial supporting analysis.

**Cases:** MED-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S12: Malaria resurgence: a systematic review and assessment of its causes](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/1475-2875-11-122), Results, Reported causes; Discussion limitations. Supports: Review denominators and evidence grading.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes review construction and its own evidence limits.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Review may miss undocumented events and attribution reflects authors' interests.

**Inference limit:** Counts cannot estimate causal frequency or compare event magnitude.

## MED-K19

The 1971 refugee-camp implementation led by Mahalanabis demonstrated family-administered oral rehydration in an emergency, extending evidence beyond tightly staffed research settings.

**Cases:** MED-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S13: Magic bullet: the history of oral rehydration therapy](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/4B8A9C4B64657965297CCE1E21C108C7/S0025727300036905a.pdf/magic_bullet_the_history_of_oral_rehydration_therapy.pdf), p. 389, Gaining momentum. Supports: Contrasts refugee-camp administration by families with Matlab monitoring.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Emergency scarcity of clinical labour -> caregiver administration -> feasible delivery outside intensive monitoring
- **strongest rival:** Only unusually selected patients/families may have made the demonstration feasible
- **discriminating observation:** Observed use with less professional supervision under actual camp conditions
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S13 explicitly contrasts staffing and administration; supports operational feasibility in that setting
- **remaining ambiguity:** Does not establish universal substitution for clinical care or later diffusion effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Emergency adoption may not predict routine household use or comparative efficacy.

**Inference limit:** Original camp report not independently audited; historical account relies partly on participants.

## MED-K20

BRAC’s 1980s programme used household demonstration, monitoring and wider community engagement; its 1993 evaluation sampled 90 villages from three programme phases. Teaching coexisted with expanding packaged-ORS distribution.

**Cases:** MED-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S14: The status of ORT in Bangladesh: how widely is it used?](https://doi.org/10.1093/heapol/12.1.58), pp. 59–60, Box 1 and Methodology. Supports: Teaching approach, worker monitoring and survey design.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes intervention organization and sample, not causal programme efficacy.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Programme-affiliated evaluation and informal transmission complicate attribution.

**Inference limit:** Reach, retained competence, reported use and health outcomes remain separate measures.

## MED-K21

Chowdhury et al. Table 2 reports 51.8%, 54.5% and 53.0% ORT use for all reported diarrhoeal episodes across cohorts; the abstract’s 60% conflicts with that denominator, while the discussion obtains over 60% after excluding dysentery.

**Cases:** MED-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S14: The status of ORT in Bangladesh: how widely is it used?](https://doi.org/10.1093/heapol/12.1.58), p. 58 abstract; p. 62 Table 2 footnote; pp. 64–65 Discussion. Supports: Direct denominator comparison; broad ORT definition includes home fluids.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Source-internal measurement audit, verified against table and discussion rather than abstract alone.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The abstract could reflect an unstated restriction rather than a transcription error.

**Inference limit:** Not strict packaged-ORS coverage or population-weighted national rate; no new pooled estimate calculated.

## MED-K22

In experiment 1 of the 2011–2012 one-city Chinese audit, expected purchase outside the hospital reduced antibiotic prescribing from 55% to 10% without a request; with a request, the corresponding rates were 85% and 14%.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §I results; §III experiment 1 protocol. Supports: Randomly assigned standardized-patient roles and reported encounter rates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Changed expected dispensing location -> changed provider incentives/signals -> prescribing response
- **strongest rival:** Outside-purchase script may signal offense, distrust or counterfeit risk
- **discriminating observation:** Demand held explicit while purchase location changes; further scripts varying alternative signals
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 paired physician design and second-experiment scripts inspected; demand effect largely disappears with outside purchase
- **remaining ambiguity:** Mechanism decomposition less secure than treatment-script effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Scripts may signal factors besides lost revenue; auditors were not blinded.

**Inference limit:** Identifies response to scripts in this sample, not national policy effects or patient health effects.

## MED-K23

The audit’s additional scripts support financial incentives as a major explanation for prescribing in its sampled encounters, while leaving general-equilibrium reform effects unresolved.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §III experiment 2 and stated results; limitations. Supports: Relative-pharmacy, knowledge and gift treatments test rival interpretations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Revenue expectation -> unnecessary prescription incentive -> observed excess use
- **strongest rival:** Etiquette, perceived demand and drug-quality concern predict some similar responses
- **discriminating observation:** Changing those signals while maintaining outside-purchase condition
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 reports persistence under relative-pharmacy script and smaller knowledge effects; original experiment examined
- **remaining ambiguity:** Alternative signals remain bundled and actual reforms change hospital budgets.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Social signaling cannot be removed perfectly; providers may substitute other services after reform.

**Inference limit:** Not an estimate of nationwide incentive removal or a complete explanation of prescribing everywhere.

## MED-K24

This prescribing experiment illustrates a possible divergence between provider revenue and appropriate use, but does not quantify resulting antimicrobial resistance or total social harm.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §I motivation; §§II–III methods. Supports: Revenue mechanism, concern about resistance and measured prescribing outcome.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Revenue incentive -> unnecessary antibiotic exposure if prescriptions used -> possible wider resistance cost
- **strongest rival:** Prescriptions may not be used or marginal ecological effects may be small in a given setting
- **discriminating observation:** Link actual dispensed use to resistance outcomes with appropriate controls
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 establishes encounter prescribing responses but collected no resistance outcomes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Private incentive divergence supported; ecological magnitude and welfare balance unmeasured.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Needed treatment can also be underprovided, and prescription does not prove ingestion.

**Inference limit:** Externality is a mechanism-level interpretation, not a measured outcome of these visits.

## MOB-K01

ORBIS models Roman connectivity as a seasonally variable combination of road, river and sea routes; its route outputs are modeled opportunities rather than observed adoption or actual journeys.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S01: ORBIS: The Stanford Geospatial Network Model of the Roman World, version dated 2 May 2012](https://orbis.stanford.edu/orbis2012/ORBIS_v1paper_20120501.pdf), pp. 7–11 and 19. Supports: Authors define average structural simulations, seasonal constraints and non-contemporaneous road coverage.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Local knowledge, secondary roads and route-specific hazards can change actual decisions.

**Inference limit:** A network model cannot establish traffic shares, willingness to adopt, welfare or the causal contribution of Roman state road building.

## MOB-K02

For pottery movement in lowland Britain c. 250–400 CE, Wiseman and colleagues infer best-fit road costs roughly three times river and four times sea costs, with wide uncertainty and regional river variation.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S02: The costs of transporting goods by different modes: A case study of pottery movement in late Roman Britain](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/7291f9ad-f581-44cb-9e21-a63889ffd40b/content), abstract; Table 3 p. 8; §5 p. 10. Supports: Estimated ratios are derived from excavated pottery distributions and a transport network.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Transport/transfer costs vary by mode -> buyers face different delivered costs -> pottery demand declines with generalized distance -> observed distribution fits moderate modal ratios.
- **strongest rival:** Institutional supply, product preference or distribution hubs generate the same spatial decay without the assumed cost-demand relationship.
- **discriminating observation:** Independent consignment/freight accounts or repeated fits across commodities and regions with different social distributions would separate general costs from pottery-specific institutions.
- **evidence sought found:** Read MOB-S02 §1.1, Table 3 and §§4.4–5: fits, river variation and rural/military exclusions support a bounded inference, not direct traffic measurement.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Cost is inferred from the outcome to be explained; network and demand assumptions leave multiple explanations possible.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Text-based Mediterranean estimates can imply much greater water advantages; the authors argue these often compare unlike segments and omit handling costs.

**Inference limit:** Rural pottery distribution, not all goods or military movement; the cost-demand model, sample and inferred routes matter. Abstract and conclusion summarize river ranges differently; no precise interval is generalized here.

## MOB-K03

Segment-level freight prices can misstate door-to-door modal advantages when transshipment, storage, port charges and access legs are omitted.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S02: The costs of transporting goods by different modes: A case study of pottery movement in late Roman Britain](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/7291f9ad-f581-44cb-9e21-a63889ffd40b/content), §1.1 pp. 1–2 and §4.4 pp. 8–9. Supports: Explicit chain of transport costs and critique of comparisons omitting transfers.
- [MOB-S01: ORBIS: The Stanford Geospatial Network Model of the Roman World, version dated 2 May 2012](https://orbis.stanford.edu/orbis2012/ORBIS_v1paper_20120501.pdf), p. 7 and p. 17. Supports: ORBIS warns expense data are schematic and uses a price-edict sailing-time calibration.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Additional transfers/access legs incur resources -> total delivery cost differs from line-haul price -> ranking inferred from a segment may change.
- **strongest rival:** Sea advantages remain dominant because transfer costs are negligible relative to long line-haul savings.
- **discriminating observation:** Observe or reconstruct full chains at different distances; whether adding transfers changes relative delivered cost distinguishes claims for a particular route.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S02 explicitly enumerates omitted costs and derives different end-to-end ratios; MOB-S01 acknowledges schematic price calibration.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The existence of omitted terms is established, but no universal ranking reversal is demonstrated.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Long bulk sea voyages can retain a large advantage despite transfer costs.

**Inference limit:** This is a measurement/mechanism proposition; it does not imply Roman land transport was universally cheaper or universally preferred.

## MOB-K04

Inka expansion incorporated earlier Andean routes and added or maintained roads, stores and wayside facilities, so imperial scaling was partly the integration of inherited infrastructure.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S03: Qhapaq Ñan, Andean Road System (No. 1459): ICOMOS advisory body evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/document/152728), printed pp. 321 and 324–325, Description and History. Supports: Lists road engineering and supply structures and states that much of the network preceded Inka occupation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The evaluation also describes new secondary roads; inheritance does not mean no technical innovation or expansion.

**Inference limit:** Heritage synthesis, not a dated census of every route. No single inventor or uniform date of network completion is assigned.

## MOB-K05

For Garrido’s Cachiyuyo de Llampos road segment in the Atacama, water and forage scarcity make predominantly pedestrian traffic more plausible than regular llama caravans.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MOB-S04: Rethinking imperial infrastructure: A bottom-up perspective on the Inca Road](https://www.academia.edu/26536064/Rethinking_Imperial_Infrastructure_A_bottom_up_perspective_on_the_Inca_Road), §4.1, pp. 98–99. Supports: Survey geography, scarce water, absent vegetation and alternative highland pasture routes motivate the author’s inference.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sparse water/forage -> higher logistical burden for llama caravans -> travelers favor pedestrian traffic on this segment.
- **strongest rival:** Organized supply or small occasional caravans allowed substantial pack-animal traffic despite local scarcity.
- **discriminating observation:** Road-associated corrals, dung, feed provision or transport-specific evidence would discriminate better than generic camelid food bones.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S04 §4.1 reports ecology, limited water and absent ethnographic caravan precedent for this locality; no transport census was found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Ecological feasibility and alternative-route reasoning do not establish actual traffic shares; preservation/recognition of animal evidence must be tested.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Llama transport occurred elsewhere in the Andes; occasional camelid use on this segment is not excluded.

**Inference limit:** Traffic composition is inferred, not counted. This supports ecological task differentiation rather than a claim that Inka roads generally lacked pack-animal traffic.

## MOB-K06

At Cachiyuyo de Llampos, settlement, craft and roadside assemblages support Garrido’s interpretation that some local miners used the Inka Road for exchange beyond directly administered state production.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S04: Rethinking imperial infrastructure: A bottom-up perspective on the Inca Road](https://www.academia.edu/26536064/Rethinking_Imperial_Infrastructure_A_bottom_up_perspective_on_the_Inca_Road), §§4–6, especially pp. 100–106. Supports: Mining camps concentrated nearer the road; non-Inka roadside craft activity and contrast with official Tambo Medanoso inform the interpretation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial road creates traffic/supply opportunities -> local miners concentrate nearer it and build craft/exchange spaces -> production connects to wider circulation.
- **strongest rival:** State directly organized miners or supervised exchange informally while leaving little formal architecture.
- **discriminating observation:** Uniform official storage/consumption and elite production supervision at mining camps would favor direct control; contrasting domestic organization and nonofficial roadside craft activity favor autonomy.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S04 §§4–6 compares camp layout, ceramic/food assemblages and craft traces with Tambo Medanoso; found meaningful contrasts and roadside non-Inka craft sites.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Indirect taxation, permissions or local intermediaries can coexist with the observed household organization.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Substantial state-controlled mining and corvée systems existed in other locations discussed by the same author.

**Inference limit:** Absence of elite architecture is not proof of wholly autonomous exchange; site chronology, informal supervision and local intermediaries remain alternatives.

## MOB-K07

Early private Indian railway expansion combined British capital with colonial guarantees, while government line-placement priorities included military control; observed construction was not simply a market vote by passengers or farmers.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S06: Regulation, Ownership and Costs: A Historical Perspective from Indian Railways, April 2011 version](https://idei.fr/sites/default/files/medias/doc/conf/icied/papers_2011/bogartbis.pdf), §2.1, pp. 6–7. Supports: Concession contracts and 5% capital guarantee at a fixed exchange rate; payments when returns fell short.
- [MOB-S05: Railroads of the Raj: Estimating the Impact of Transportation Infrastructure](https://dave-donaldson.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Donaldson_RRRaj_AER.pdf), §I.C, pp. 905–907. Supports: Government line placement and military priorities using historical planning documents.
- [MOB-S07: Extractive institutions? Investor returns to Indian railway companies in the age of high imperialism, April 2019 accepted manuscript](https://sites.socsci.uci.edu/~dbogart/extractive_copyacceptedJIE-March2019.pdf), p. 2. Supports: Guarantee shortfalls were funded from Government of India tax revenues.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Risky capital project and colonial strategic priorities -> guaranteed concessions and state routing -> financed trunk-line deployment.
- **strongest rival:** Comparable rail expansion would have occurred without guarantees because expected commercial demand alone attracted capital.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable unguaranteed proposals, financing failures and decision correspondence predating construction would help separate risk support from redundant subsidy.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S06 describes contract terms and contemporaries disputing necessity; MOB-S05 reconstructs military priorities; MOB-S07 confirms taxpayer-funded shortfalls.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Documents establish instruments and objectives, not the counterfactual size/timing of a fully unguaranteed network.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Trade and humanitarian motives also mattered; private capital and local demand were consequential.

**Inference limit:** Historians’ reconstructions of planning and contracts, not independent inspection here of every concession or Dalhousie minute. No claim that all lines had identical motives.

## MOB-K08

Donaldson’s published study finds lower trade costs and higher trade, and estimates a 0.164 log-point rise in real agricultural income per unit area following rail access in its district panel; the paper summarizes the income effect as about 16%.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S05: Railroads of the Raj: Estimating the Impact of Transportation Infrastructure](https://dave-donaldson.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Donaldson_RRRaj_AER.pdf), §§III–V; §V.B–D pp. 923–926 and Table 4. Supports: Price/trade analysis and within-district panel of 192 districts, 1870–1930; unbuilt-line placebo checks address selection.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Rail access reduces trade costs -> price gaps narrow and trade expands -> districts specialize/access cheaper goods -> measured real agricultural income rises.
- **strongest rival:** Railroads were built where income/trade would already have grown, or unrelated investment caused both access and growth.
- **discriminating observation:** If unbuilt but seriously planned lines predict similar income growth, route-selection bias would undermine the rail mechanism; commodity-price and network-cost responses provide additional pathway evidence.
- **evidence sought found:** Read MOB-S05 §§III–V and Table 4: salt-price/network and trade analyses plus over 40,000 km of planned-unbuilt routes show no analogous placebo effects in the reported tests.
- **remaining ambiguity:** No randomized assignment; plans need not have identical latent trends, and distributional/nonagricultural effects are outside the main outcome.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Placement was nonrandom; model assumptions and residual unobserved local trends can affect causal interpretation.

**Inference limit:** Agricultural output valued at local prices and deflated, divided by land area: not household disposable income or a measure of distribution. 0.164 log points is the reported coefficient, not an exact 16% transformation.

## MOB-K09

Bogart and Chaudhary find lower working expenses after colonial state takeovers of Indian railways, with employment reductions supporting a labour-cost channel; they do not directly observe railway wages.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S06: Regulation, Ownership and Costs: A Historical Perspective from Indian Railways, April 2011 version](https://idei.fr/sites/default/files/medias/doc/conf/icied/papers_2011/bogartbis.pdf), §§4–6, especially pp. 22–25 and Tables 7–9. Supports: Predetermined contractual purchase dates and within-railway estimates; employment and expense-category evidence; direct wage-data limitation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Contractual state purchase strengthens treasury incentive -> labour/input reorganization -> lower employment/expenses.
- **strongest rival:** Different reporting, anticipated retrenchment or deteriorating service generates an apparent cost reduction.
- **discriminating observation:** Expense falls after rather than before scheduled purchases, across consistently reported categories, with employment changes and no measured accident deterioration.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S06 checks timing/accounting and selected quality measures; Tables 7–9 show expense and employment patterns; authors explicitly lack wage data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Unmeasured service quality and other organizational changes may matter; worker welfare cannot be inferred from firm costs.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Their measured safety/quality checks do not cover all service dimensions; institutional differences limit transfer to other nationalizations.

**Inference limit:** April 2011 version used; no mixed-version numerical effect. Fiscal savings cannot be equated with workers’ welfare, and reduced wage rates are not established.

## MOB-K10

The April 2019 accepted manuscript by Bogart and Chaudhary estimates average annual total returns of 3.7% on Indian guaranteed railway securities for 1880–1908 and finds no average abnormal returns in the three years before state purchases.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S07: Extractive institutions? Investor returns to Indian railway companies in the age of high imperialism, April 2019 accepted manuscript](https://sites.socsci.uci.edu/~dbogart/extractive_copyacceptedJIE-March2019.pdf), p. 3; Table 2a and takeover event-study discussion. Supports: Clearly defines guaranteed securities, period, return comparison and event-study result.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Guaranteed payments still exposed Indian tax revenues; ordinary returns at secondary-market prices can coexist with other transfers, coercion or opportunity costs.

**Inference limit:** This challenges a particular excess-investor-return mechanism, not the broader possibility of colonial extraction or unequal infrastructure benefits.

## MOB-K11

The Ghanaian western railway was routed toward gold mines and Kumasi for commercial and military purposes, while the eastern route had different political and commercial origins and reached Kumasi only in 1923 after wartime delays.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.1, printed pp. 5–6. Supports: Reconstructs western line Sekondi–Tarkwa–Obuasi–Kumasi and eastern Accra route, lobbying, governors and construction dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Alternative surveyed routes were judged attractive for agriculture; cocoa potential was already relevant to the eastern line.

**Inference limit:** Mainly the authors’ historical reconstruction; colonial political interests and technical transport benefits are not mutually exclusive.

## MOB-K12

Jedwab and Moradi find that railway access encouraged cocoa production and settlement growth in Ghana, using geographic controls, planned-but-unbuilt lines and instruments to address route selection.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §§3–5, printed pp. 9–20. Supports: Spatial data and identification exercises compare rail areas with controls and placebo routes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Rail access lowers inland shipment costs -> farmers expand cocoa -> rural production supports trading towns.
- **strongest rival:** Planners selected inherently promising cocoa areas and pre-existing settlement growth rather than causing expansion.
- **discriminating observation:** Effects absent near comparable planned-but-unbuilt lines, stronger with actual access, and robust to alternative alignments would count against simple selection.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §4 and Table 3 compare planned routes, geographic controls and straight-line instruments; paper reports rail effects and weak placebo effects for Ghana.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Instrument exclusion is substantive; political endpoint choice and cocoa suitability can still correlate with unobserved potential.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The eastern line could partly respond to cocoa prospects; instruments rely on exclusion assumptions and local growth can include relocation.

**Inference limit:** This is the January 2015 forthcoming version. Effects on cells and towns do not by themselves identify household gains or a full national cost-benefit balance.

## MOB-K13

Ghanaian railway traffic declined sharply after 1974, yet towns near former railway routes retained relative economic importance through the study’s 2000 endpoint as roads expanded.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.2 pp. 7–8 and §6 pp. 20–26. Supports: Rail decline, near-route road replacement, and persistence results; authors discuss sunk investments and coordination.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Early rail connection concentrates settlement/investment -> subsequent actors coordinate around established towns -> relative concentration persists after rail traffic declines.
- **strongest rival:** Persistent geography, road replacement or unmeasured durable amenities explain persistence without expectation-based coordination.
- **discriminating observation:** Persistence after equalized access and controls for inherited assets supports coordination; direct evidence of investors following prior settlement rather than amenities would be stronger.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §6 controls for roads and historical facilities and discusses population persistence. Authors explicitly note possible mismeasurement of sunk assets and instrument caveats.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The fall of rail does not remove roads or all place advantages; evidence supports persistence more strongly than a uniquely identified coordination mechanism.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Roads preserved absolute transport access; persistence does not show towns could flourish with no replacement transport.

**Inference limit:** Not a claim about current Ghanaian railways. Sunk capital, continuing amenities and expectation-based coordination are difficult to fully disentangle.

## MOB-K14

The same Ghana study describes a feedback between poor service, lower freight revenue and deferred maintenance, alongside cocoa cultivation’s movement into new forest areas, as context for rail decline.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.2, printed pp. 7–8. Supports: Historical description links deficits/service/maintenance and changing cocoa geography; forests converted to cocoa and later food crops.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Service/financial deterioration and shifting crop geography -> lower rail demand/revenue -> deferred repair -> further deterioration.
- **strongest rival:** Road competition or political mismanagement alone caused decline, with maintenance and crop shifts mainly consequences.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated line-level service, finance, repair and commodity-flow series could establish ordering and distinguish shocks.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §2.2 provides historical sequence and mechanisms, including forest-to-cocoa shifts, but no separate causal decomposition was inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Feedback makes direction reciprocal; environmental net costs are not identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Political instability, roads, changing labour costs and management also mattered.

**Inference limit:** The paper does not separately identify the causal contribution of each decline mechanism or quantify net deforestation, deaths, dispossession or environmental welfare.

## MOB-K15

The 1897 New York electric-cab venture deliberately sold transport service while retaining vehicles and specialist control, rather than merely selling cars to inexperienced owners; operation was demonstrated before profitability was established.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), pp. 306–307. Supports: Morris and Salom’s service model, operating experience and initial-cost assessment.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The first six months were probably loss-making on Kirsch’s reconstruction, and reported operation is not proof of scalable profitability.

**Inference limit:** Operational results were originally self-reported and the profitability inference uses later cost studies; this contribution does not reproduce the implied cost calculation.

## MOB-K16

Kirsch’s history attributes the EVC’s breakdown to a bundle of production, financial and organizational problems, and distinguishes early operating-company failures from the parent’s default in December 1907.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), p. 307. Supports: Production delays, fires, shareholder suits, public attacks, regional failures and later parent default.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Ambitious system expansion encounters operational/financial problems -> operating firms fail -> organizational collapse damages the service model.
- **strongest rival:** Battery shortcomings alone made profitable service impossible, so financial trouble was simply a downstream symptom.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable fleets with sound finance but similar batteries, plus separated operating/financing accounts, would test sufficiency of the battery explanation.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S09 p. 307 documents several failure modes; MOB-S10 shows later task-specific viable fleets, weakening a timeless impossibility claim but not isolating EVC causes.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Later fleets had improved batteries and different tasks; sources share some intellectual lineage; no clean comparison quantifies causal weights.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Battery limitations and unreliable vehicles remain part of the contemporary feasibility problem; business failure does not prove a viable alternative would otherwise have won.

**Inference limit:** Author dissertation summary; relative weights of factors are not independently identified. A successful EVC counterfactual is not an observed outcome.

## MOB-K17

Mom’s comparative history distinguishes early battery-related failures from later electric fleets that worked under specific duty cycles and disciplined maintenance; it rejects the electric starter as a sufficient explanation for all electric-vehicle displacement.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S10: Das Scheitern des frühen Elektromobils (1895–1925): Versuch einer Neubewertung](https://zeithistorische-forschungen.de/sites/default/files/medien/material/2017-3/Mom_1997.pdf), pp. 281–283, conclusions. Supports: Discusses sulphation/maintenance, commercial and specialized fleets, universal gasoline use and starter timing.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Duty cycle and disciplined maintenance mitigate battery weaknesses -> specified fleet work remains viable -> general private-car displacement coexists with electric niches.
- **strongest rival:** One universally inferior propulsion technology or the 1912 starter alone explains all electric decline.
- **discriminating observation:** Successful electric tasks after the starter and differing decline by application contradict a universal single-trigger prediction.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S10 pp. 281–283 identifies successful defined fleets and notes the Model T starter appeared in 1919; source explicitly disaggregates generations/tasks.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Fleet success does not establish equal suitability for touring or lower costs for every owner; the broad interpretation is historical, not an experiment.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The gasoline vehicle’s range, autonomy and purchase cost were real advantages for general private use; fleet successes were conditional.

**Inference limit:** US-focused case with European task contrasts. No all-vehicle market share or claim of equivalent generalized performance; interpretation translated from German.

## MOB-K18

The Electric Vehicle Association of America, established in 1909, promoted utility fleets, charging facilities, off-peak rates and a common charging plug; this was an organized attempt to build complements after early electric-car setbacks.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), pp. 308–309. Supports: Formation, membership, marketing and standard-plug committee, then incorporation into NELA in 1916.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Central stations were not uniformly supportive, and coordination alone did not remove battery and vehicle costs.

**Inference limit:** Earlier coordination changing the dominant technology is Kirsch’s counterfactual hypothesis, not a causal result established here.

## MOB-K19

Postwar container scaling required compatible handling equipment and intermodal facilities; the published trade study distinguishes 1956 US commercial deployment from the start of its international diffusion period in 1966.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), §§2.1–2.3, pp. 38–40. Supports: Specialized cranes, road/rail/ship integration, Ideal-X deployment and international standardization narrative.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Compatible boxes and equipment reduce repeated handling -> ship/rail/truck interfaces work together -> deployments can scale beyond one proprietary route.
- **strongest rival:** A better ship or cheaper box alone explains diffusion without complementary investment.
- **discriminating observation:** Early experiments that failed with weak handling complements, followed by adoption tied to cranes/terminals/standards, discriminate against artifact-only explanations.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 §§2.1–2.3 reports prewar experiments, specialized-capital constraints, early port reluctance and negotiated compatibility.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Narrative is inherited from earlier histories; it does not prove any one exact standard or institutional arrangement was necessary.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Earlier containers and domestic experiments existed; some goods and routes remained outside containerization.

**Inference limit:** Dates describe this study’s modern container-system convention, not invention of boxes or the first transport container anywhere.

## MOB-K20

US construction subsidies tied to approved container dimensions, international standardization and military logistics demand in the Vietnam War helped create conditions for container diffusion; these were distinct from intrinsic box efficiency.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), §2.3, p. 40. Supports: Federal Maritime Board policy, ISO compromises and military logistics described as complementary mechanisms.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Subsidy specifications/ISO coordination and military demand -> reduce compatibility/investment uncertainty and add traffic -> support diffusion.
- **strongest rival:** Commercial demand and private integrated shipping would have produced the same diffusion trajectory without these policies.
- **discriminating observation:** Route- and firm-level investment responses to policy changes, or comparable commercial routes without military demand, would separate contribution from coincidence.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 p. 40 documents the historical policy and procurement channels; no dedicated causal estimate of their incremental effect is provided.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Military demand, private innovation and standardization interacted; narrative timing cannot identify necessity.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The historical narrative also starts with private shipping investment; it does not identify military demand as necessary for every route.

**Inference limit:** Historical interpretation in an econometric paper, based on prior histories. No causal estimate of each policy or claim that war was necessary for global containerization.

## MOB-K21

Bernhofen and colleagues warn that very large estimates using adoption dates alone do not convincingly establish causal trade effects; their product-containerizability comparisons are smaller and provide stronger but still qualified support.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), p. 37; Table 2 pp. 43–44; §3.4 pp. 45–46. Supports: Pre-trend failures and endogeneity warning; benchmark 17.4% North–North product differential and approximately 14% world differential with delayed accrual.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Container systems lower relevant handling costs -> suitable products should gain more trade than unsuitable products after adoption.
- **strongest rival:** Countries adopt while trade is already growing, inflating aggregate adoption-timing effects.
- **discriminating observation:** Pre-adoption trends among future adopters and suitable/unsuitable products test timing confounding; comparative product responses add a discriminating dimension.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 p. 37 explicitly reports failed parallel-trend robustness for adoption-only models. Tables 2–3 and pp. 44–46 give smaller product differentials and classification sensitivity.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Suitability definitions and correlated product shocks remain; product effects are not equivalent to all general-equilibrium benefits.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Classification and robustness choices alter estimated magnitudes; product-level effects do not capture every network spillover.

**Inference limit:** Do not quote old working-paper 700% figures as established causal effects. Denominators are bilateral product trade comparisons over specified periods, not total global GDP or a universal return to a container port.

## MOB-K22

The 1960 West Coast mechanization agreement exchanged union acceptance of labour-saving changes for a $29 million fund over 1961–1966, and its financing imposed uneven charges contested by Volkswagen.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S12: Volkswagenwerk Aktiengesellschaft v. Federal Maritime Commission, 390 U.S. 261](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/390/261), opening paragraphs 2–4, 390 U.S. 261. Supports: Agreement, fund, and weight-versus-volume assessment dispute.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** A negotiated fund does not establish that every worker or shipper was compensated, nor that all members regarded the terms as fair.

**Inference limit:** Court record of specific contracts and litigated incidence; not an aggregate estimate of gains or proof that containers alone caused every negotiated change.

## MOB-K23

Containerization shifted much stuffing and unstuffing away from docks, creating conflict between longshore and Teamster jurisdictions after the initial West Coast mechanization bargain.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S13: California Cartage Co. v. NLRB, 822 F.2d 1203](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/822/1203/373792/), part I, paragraphs describing break-bulk, 1960 agreement and off-dock container work. Supports: Court recounts Board findings of greater-than-expected work loss and the dispute over a fifty-mile work-preservation agreement.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Sealed intermodal boxes bypass repeated dock handling -> stuffing/unstuffing moves inland -> occupational jurisdiction is contested.
- **strongest rival:** Work loss consists only of machine substitution, with no material relocation across employers or unions.
- **discriminating observation:** Observed off-dock stuffing by Teamsters and disputes over those tasks distinguish relocation from pure disappearance.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S13 part I recounts off-dock work and the fifty-mile supplement litigation, alongside new dockside equipment duties.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Court evidence cannot count net jobs or infer conditions at every freight station.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some longshore workers acquired new container-handling roles; moving tasks is not equivalent to eliminating all employment.

**Inference limit:** Judicial retrospective of a particular dispute, not a comprehensive labour census. Legal characterization of work restrictions is not adopted as a neutral welfare assessment.

## MOB-K24

Goh Chok Tong’s 2000 terminal-opening speech records Singapore’s 1972 container berths and presents further port expansion as a coordinated choice involving scarce waterfront land, long planning horizons, workforce upgrading and information services.

**Cases:** MOB-C07. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S14: Speech by Prime Minister Goh Chok Tong at the opening of the Pasir Panjang Terminal, 30 March 2000](https://www.nas.gov.sg/archivesonline/data/pdfdoc/2000033003/gct20000330l.pdf), paragraphs 3–6, PDF pp. 1–2. Supports: 1972 chronology and official explanation of Pasir Panjang land, capacity and service choices.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The official account celebrates success and omits a full account of displaced uses, residents and environmental costs.

**Inference limit:** Strong evidence of publicly stated state rationale, weaker evidence of exclusive causes of success; no independent causal estimate and no use of the speech’s forecasts.

## NET-K01

Bloom's historical synthesis places papermaking in Baghdad by the late eighth century and its transmission to Mediterranean Christians by the twelfth; this is a broad chronology of manufacture, not a unique invention event.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S01: Papermaking: The Historical Diffusion of an Ancient Technique](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-44654-7_3), pp. 51–54, The Early Diffusion of Papermaking and The Replacement of Papyrus and Parchment. Supports: Manufacturing and transfer chronology; paper use predates European typography.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Regional practice and surviving evidence are uneven; exact first mills and routes are less secure than the broad sequence.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish a single battle-mediated transfer, rates of uptake, literacy effects, or indigenous manufacturing wherever paper was used.

## NET-K02

For the high-quality European book/writing-paper workflow reconstructed for 1300–1800, water quality, fibre preparation and gelatin sizing affected usable sheet properties; a generic recipe for pulp did not capture the complete craft.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S02: European Papermaking Techniques 1300–1800](https://paper.lib.uiowa.edu/european.php), Introduction; Raw Materials/Water; Sizing, paragraphs beginning As a result and Gelatin size allowed. Supports: Production constraints, sizing function, practical and historical evidential limits.
- [NET-S03: Material properties of Islamic paper](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/1527462/1/2016%20Mahgoub%20HS.pdf), pp. 2–3 and 9. Supports: Storage, provenance and material-classification limits prevent a simple cross-regional superiority inference.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Controlled inputs and finishing -> workable, ink-resistant sheets -> suitability for specific writing/printing tasks
- **strongest rival:** Observed surviving-paper quality could instead reflect storage and preservation selection; cheaper rough paper could satisfy other tasks.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched historical papers or workshop records separating manufacture from storage and end-use requirements.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S02 was read for process evidence; NET-S03 explicitly warns that unknown storage prevents general causal claims about stability.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Technical functionality is supported; contribution to historical diffusion and cross-regional cost differences remains unidentified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Barrett explicitly calls aspects reconstruction/hypothesis; climatic storage and survival also affect present condition.

**Inference limit:** No quantified contribution to historical costs, output or regional adoption; high-quality European paper is not all paper.

## NET-K03

Bloom cites Italian watermarked paper used in a Tunisian royal letter in 1350 and a 1409 Ibn Marzuq ruling permitting writing on Christian-made paper with objectionable watermark imagery; imported paper could support local writing without local manufacture.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S01: Papermaking: The Historical Diffusion of an Ancient Technique](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-44654-7_3), pp. 63–64, Trade Relations and the Decline of Arab Papermaking. Supports: Specific imported-paper examples and the permissive ruling.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imported sheets encounter religious objections -> jurist authorises their use -> a permissible route to continued local writing
- **strongest rival:** Material availability or price alone determines purchases; the ruling retrospectively validates an already established practice.
- **discriminating observation:** Purchasing changes before/after the ruling or correspondence showing objections prevented use until resolved.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S01 reports both actual imported-paper use and the later ruling; no before/after purchase series was found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Chronological sequence demonstrates compatibility of religion and adoption, but not the ruling's marginal effect on demand.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The ruling records a normative solution, not the subsequent number of purchasers; imports and local production could coexist.

**Inference limit:** Underlying letter and fatwa not directly inspected. Does not prove imports were always cheaper or that ruling caused a measured increase.

## NET-K04

Mahgoub et al.'s 228-paper collection includes 133 undated samples and mostly eighteenth-/nineteenth-century dated examples; text language and finishing cannot by themselves establish paper's place of manufacture.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S03: Material properties of Islamic paper](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/1527462/1/2016%20Mahgoub%20HS.pdf), pp. 2–3, discussion before Methods and Reference collection. Supports: Sample composition, uncertain provenance, trade and finishing complications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some watermarks, fibres and morphology can inform provenance when combined with other evidence.

**Inference limit:** The study supplies no medieval adoption curve and no representative comparison of Islamic and European production efficiency.

## NET-K05

The surviving 868 Diamond Sutra's translated colophon names Wang Jie and a distribution dedication for his parents; it documents sponsored woodblock production and declared purpose, not achieved circulation volume.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S06: Buddhist texts: The Diamond Sutra](https://idp.bl.uk/discover/learning/buddhism-on-the-silk-roads/articles/buddhism-on-the-ground/buddhist-texts-the-diamond-sutra/), The Diamond Sutra; Colophon from the Diamond Sutra (object Or.8210/P.2). Supports: Date, printing method, sponsorship and declared distribution purpose.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** This is a surviving dated object rather than the earliest-ever experiment; no run count is preserved in the cited description.

**Inference limit:** Institutionally translated colophon, not independent original-language collation. Purpose does not demonstrate that universal distribution occurred.

## NET-K06

Shen Gua describes Bi Sheng's clay-type process in 1041–1048, including type levelling, multiple common characters and alternating boards; he reports greater convenience for many copies than a few.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.
